From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I'm having some trouble at work. I'm an electrician, and lately... I just keep going back over my jobs again and again. \n\nI'll finish up, but then I have to keep checking the breakers, the labels, the torque marks. I even take photos of everything before I leave, and sometimes I'll actually drive all the way back to a site just to check it all one more time. It's getting to the point where a really simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and... well, people are starting to notice. So I figured I needed to talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exactly like that. Even after I know for a fact that I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just keeps barging in on me. I'll start thinking that maybe I missed one loose neutral wire, or... or that I left something in a way that could start a fire. I try to just shake it off and focus on the drive, but it doesn't work. The thought just follows me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really have a certain order I go in, or like a specific routine. It's not about doing it step-by-step... I just have to keep looking at things until I finally feel sure it's safe. \n\nI might check a breaker, then look at the torque marks, and then go right back to the breaker again. It's really just about trying to convince myself I didn't leave a hazard behind. It doesn't seem to matter what order I check it all in, as long as I keep checking until that bad feeling goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That's exactly it. I mean, working with electricity... if you mess up, things can catch fire. People could get hurt. \n\nSo when I'm doing all that checking, I'm just trying to make absolutely sure none of that happens. If I don't go back and look, I just feel like... like a disaster is going to happen and it'll be my fault. So I have to check to prevent it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or behaviors that you feel compelled to do over and over, outside of your work as an electrician?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... it's pretty much just at work. Like, when I'm at home, I don't really have anything like that going on. I might, you know, check that the front door is locked before bed or make sure the stove is off, but just the one time. I don't get stuck doing it over and over. It really just seems to be the electrical stuff on the job that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you're not able to perform these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I can't go back and check, I get really anxious. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in to see you. \n\nI just keep going back over these jobs again and again. Checking the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, looking at photos on my phone... sometimes I'll even drive all the way back to a site after I've left if I'm not sure. If I can't do that, the worry just eats at me until I do. But because I have to do all this checking, a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and... well, people at work are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that keep repeating and cause you distress outside of your concerns about electrical work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have anything like that. No repeating thoughts or pictures in my head about other things. Like I was saying, it really just comes down to the electrical work. Outside of my job, my mind is pretty clear and I don't have those kinds of worries.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to check things over and over at work affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. My business schedule is really slipping lately. Jobs that I should easily finish in one visit end up turning into callbacks and extra checks. It's getting to the point where it's starting to cost me client confidence, and it takes away the time I should be spending with my apprentices. \n\nAnd as for my personal time... um, yeah, I've been skipping things I used to enjoy doing after work. I just don't have the focus for them because I'm still tied up reviewing a job in my head, or I'm sitting around worrying about whether I need to go back to a site. It just kind of takes over my evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check at work need to be in a certain condition, such as switches being in a specific position, for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not about them looking a certain way or... being lined up perfectly, if that's what you mean. I don't care about that. \n\nIt's strictly about safety for me. I just need to be absolutely sure the connection is tight or the breaker is set right so there's no hazard. So, yeah, it's not about things being in a specific position just to feel okay, it's just about knowing the job was done right and won't cause a fire.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things over and over if you were absolutely certain that everything was done correctly and safely, but maybe not in the most perfect or orderly way?\n\nPatient: If I was absolutely certain? No, I wouldn't check it again. Like I said, I really don't care if the wires look pretty or if things are perfectly orderly inside the box. \n\nIf I knew for a 100 percent fact that the job was safe and wasn't going to spark or start a fire, I'd just pack up my tools and go home. The whole problem is just... I can't ever seem to feel absolutely certain about it. But if I could, no, I wouldn't need to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over would decrease if someone else, who you trust to be competent, checked them first?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. I'm usually the lead on my jobs, so I'm the one checking the apprentices' work, not the other way around. I haven't really had anyone else check behind me in a long time. \n\nI guess... maybe it would help if it was another electrician I really trusted? But honestly, knowing how I've been lately, I'd probably just start worrying that *they* missed a loose connection or something. So, yeah, I'm really not sure if that would actually stop me from checking it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious about your work if everything was perfectly symmetrical or organized, even if it didn't affect the safety of the job?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, I really don't care how it looks. Making things symmetrical or perfectly organized wouldn't do anything for me if I wasn't sure it was safe. The anxiety is entirely about the safety of the connections, not how neat they look. So no, making it look pretty wouldn't make me feel any less anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas in your life, besides electrical work, where you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or checks to feel okay?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the electrical work. I don't go back and check the locks on my house a dozen times or, you know, stare at the stove to make sure it's off. I lock the door, I leave, and I don't think about it again. It's really just when I'm on the job and dealing with those wires. Outside of work, I don't have any of those issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your work if everything was done correctly, but not perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. Like I was saying before, I'm not really looking for perfection. If by \"correct\" you mean the wiring is solid and it's completely safe, then... yeah, I'd be totally satisfied. \n\nI don't care if the electrical tape is a little messy or if the wires aren't perfectly straight inside the box. I just... I just have to be absolutely sure it's not going to spark or start a fire somewhere down the line. So, correct is really all I'm aiming for. The hard part is just convincing myself that it actually *is* correct.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over would decrease if you were able to accept that you might have made a small mistake, but one that wouldn't cause any harm?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I guess if I could actually believe that a mistake wouldn't cause any harm, then yeah, maybe I wouldn't need to check it so much. \n\nBut that's kind of the whole problem. When you're dealing with electricity, my brain just tells me that any mistake is going to cause a fire or get someone hurt. It's hard for me to even imagine a mistake in a junction box that I'd be able to just brush off as harmless. So... I don't know. If I could somehow accept that, maybe the checking would go down, but getting my head to believe it is safe is the part I'm stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check things over and over if you were certain that you had done everything correctly, but not necessarily perfectly?\n\nPatient: Well, if I was absolutely certain it was correct and safe, then no, I wouldn't keep checking it. Like I said, I don't care about it being perfect. It's not about making it look pretty. If I knew for a fact that it was wired right and wouldn't cause a fire, I'd pack up my tools and go home. The checking only happens because I can't shake that feeling that I missed something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you were convinced that your work was safe, even if it wasn't done in the most efficient or perfect way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don't care about efficiency or perfection. If I was 100 percent convinced it was safe, I'd stop checking immediately. That's the only thing that matters to me—just knowing it won't cause a fire. The messiness isn't the issue for me, it's just the fear of it being dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Would your need to check things over and over decrease if someone else checked them first and confirmed they were safe, even if not perfectly organized?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I haven't really had anyone else check behind me in a long time. \n\nI guess... maybe it would help if it was another electrician I really trusted? But honestly, knowing how I've been lately, I'd probably just start worrying that *they* missed a loose connection or something. So, yeah, I'm really not sure if that would actually stop me from checking it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious about your work if everything was perfectly symmetrical or organized, even if it didn't affect the safety of the job?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, I really don't care how it looks. Making things symmetrical or perfectly organized wouldn't do anything for me if I wasn't sure it was safe. The anxiety is entirely about the safety of the connections, not how neat they look. So no, making it look pretty wouldn't make me feel any less anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas in your life, besides electrical work, where you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or checks to feel okay?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the electrical work. I don't go back and check the locks on my house a dozen times or, you know, stare at the stove to make sure it's off. I lock the door, I leave, and I don't think about it again. It's really just when I'm on the job and dealing with those wires. Outside of work, I don't have any of those issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your work if everything was done correctly, but not perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. Like I was saying before, I'm not really looking for perfection. If by \"correct\" you mean the wiring is solid and it's completely safe, then... yeah, I'd be totally satisfied. \n\nI don't care if the electrical tape is a little messy or if the wires aren't perfectly straight inside the box. I just... I just have to be absolutely sure it's not going to spark or start a fire somewhere down the line. So, correct is really all I'm aiming for. The hard part is just convincing myself that it actually *is* correct.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over would decrease if you were able to accept that you might have made a small mistake, but one that wouldn't cause any harm?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I guess if I could actually believe that a mistake wouldn't cause any harm, then yeah, maybe I wouldn't need to check it so much. \n\nBut that's kind of the whole problem. When you're dealing with electricity, my brain just tells me that any mistake is going to cause a fire or get someone hurt. It's hard for me to even imagine a mistake in a junction box that I'd be able to just brush off as harmless. So... I don't know. If I could somehow accept that, maybe the checking would go down, but getting my head to believe it is safe is the part I'm stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check things over and over if you were certain that you had done everything correctly, but not necessarily perfectly?\n\nPatient: Well, if I was absolutely certain it was correct and safe, then no, I wouldn't keep checking it. Like I said, I don't care about it being perfect. It's not about making it look pretty. If I knew for a fact that it was wired right and wouldn't cause a fire, I'd pack up my tools and go home. The checking only happens because I can't shake that feeling that I missed something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you were convinced that your work was safe, even if it wasn't done in the most efficient or perfect way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don't care about efficiency or perfection. If I was 100 percent convinced it was safe, I'd stop checking immediately. That's the only thing that matters to me—just knowing it won't cause a fire. If I had that guarantee, I wouldn't look at it twice.\n\nDoctor: Would your need to check things over and over decrease if the electrical components were organized in a messy but safe way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it would. I mean... I think that's kind of what I was just getting at. How it looks really doesn't matter to me at all. It could be a complete rat's nest of wires in there, and as long as I knew for an absolute fact that it was safe and wouldn't spark... yeah, I wouldn't need to keep checking it. The messiness isn't the issue for me, it's just the fear of it being dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Would your need to check things over and over decrease if someone else checked them first and confirmed they were safe, even if not perfectly organized?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I usually work alone on my jobs, so I haven't really had a situation where someone else comes in behind me to check my work. I'm not sure if just hearing another guy say it's safe would be enough to stop me from looking at it myself. I just haven't really had that happen, so it's hard to say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I even take photos of everything before I leave, and sometimes I'll actually drive all the way back to a site just to check it all one more time. It's getting to the point where a really simple install is taking me way longer than it should, and... well, people are starting to notice. So I figured I needed to talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exactly like that. Even after I know for a fact that I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just keeps barging in on me. I'll start thinking that maybe I missed one loose neutral wire, or... or that I left something in a way that could start a fire. I try to just shake it off and focus on the drive, but it doesn't work. The thought just follows me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really have a certain order I go in, or like a specific routine. It's not about doing it step-by-step... I just have to keep looking at things until I finally feel sure it's safe. \n\nI might check a breaker, then look at the torque marks, and then go right back to the breaker again. It's really just about trying to convince myself I didn't leave a hazard behind. It doesn't seem to matter what order I check it all in, as long as I keep checking until that bad feeling goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That's exactly it. I mean, working with electricity... if you mess up, things can catch fire. People could get hurt. \n\nSo when I'm doing all that checking, I'm just trying to make absolutely sure none of that happens. If I don't go back and look, I just feel like... like a disaster is going to happen and it'll be my fault. So I have to check to prevent it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or behaviors that you feel compelled to do over and over, outside of your work as an electrician?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... it's pretty much just at work. Like, when I'm at home, I don't really have anything like that going on. I might, you know, check that the front door is locked before bed or make sure the stove is off, but just the one time. I don't get stuck doing it over and over. It really just seems to be the electrical stuff on the job that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you're not able to perform these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I can't go back and check, I get really anxious. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in to see you. \n\nI just keep going back over these jobs again and again. Checking the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, looking at photos on my phone... sometimes I'll even drive all the way back to a site after I've left if I'm not sure. If I can't do that, the worry just eats at me until I do. But because I have to do all this checking, a simple install ends up taking way longer than it should, and... well, people at work are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or mental images that keep repeating and cause you distress outside of your concerns about electrical work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have anything like that. No repeating thoughts or pictures in my head about other things. Like I was saying, it really just comes down to the electrical work. Outside of my job, my mind is pretty clear and I don't have those kinds of worries.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to check things over and over at work affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. My business schedule is really slipping lately. Jobs that I should easily finish in one visit end up turning into callbacks and extra checks. It's getting to the point where it's starting to cost me client confidence, and it takes away the time I should be spending with my apprentices. \n\nAnd as for my personal time... um, yeah, I've been skipping things I used to enjoy doing after work. I just don't have the focus for them because I'm still tied up reviewing a job in my head, or I'm sitting around worrying about whether I need to go back to a site. It just kind of takes over my evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check at work need to be in a certain condition, such as switches being in a specific position, for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not about them looking a certain way or... being lined up perfectly, if that's what you mean. I don't care about that. \n\nIt's strictly about safety for me. I just need to be absolutely sure the connection is tight or the breaker is set right so there's no hazard. So, yeah, it's not about things being in a specific position just to feel okay, it's just about knowing the job was done right and won't cause a fire.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things over and over if you were absolutely certain that everything was done correctly and safely, but maybe not in the most perfect or orderly way?\n\nPatient: If I was absolutely certain? No, I wouldn't check it again. Like I said, I really don't care if the wires look pretty or if things are perfectly orderly inside the box. \n\nIf I knew for a 100 percent fact that the job was safe and wasn't going to spark or start a fire, I'd just pack up my tools and go home. The whole problem is just... I can't ever seem to feel absolutely certain about it. But if I could, no, I wouldn't need to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over would decrease if someone else, who you trust to be competent, checked them first?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, to be honest. I'm usually the lead on my jobs, so I'm the one checking the apprentices' work, not the other way around. I haven't really had anyone else check behind me in a long time. \n\nI guess... maybe it would help if it was another electrician I really trusted? But honestly, knowing how I've been lately, I'd probably just start worrying that *they* missed a loose connection or something. So, yeah, I'm really not sure if that would actually stop me from checking it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious about your work if everything was perfectly symmetrical or organized, even if it didn't affect the safety of the job?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I said before, I really don't care how it looks. Making things symmetrical or perfectly organized wouldn't do anything for me if I wasn't sure it was safe. The anxiety is entirely about the safety of the connections, not how neat they look. So no, making it look pretty wouldn't make me feel any less anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas in your life, besides electrical work, where you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or checks to feel okay?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the electrical work. I don't go back and check the locks on my house a dozen times or, you know, stare at the stove to make sure it's off. I lock the door, I leave, and I don't think about it again. It's really just when I'm on the job and dealing with those wires. Outside of work, I don't have any of those issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your work if everything was done correctly, but not perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. Like I was saying before, I'm not really looking for perfection. If by \"correct\" you mean the wiring is solid and it's completely safe, then... yeah, I'd be totally satisfied. \n\nI don't care if the electrical tape is a little messy or if the wires aren't perfectly straight inside the box. I just... I just have to be absolutely sure it's not going to spark or start a fire somewhere down the line. So, correct is really all I'm aiming for. The hard part is just convincing myself that it actually *is* correct.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over would decrease if you were able to accept that you might have made a small mistake, but one that wouldn't cause any harm?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I guess if I could actually believe that a mistake wouldn't cause any harm, then yeah, maybe I wouldn't need to check it so much. \n\nBut that's kind of the whole problem. When you're dealing with electricity, my brain just tells me that any mistake is going to cause a fire or get someone hurt. It's hard for me to even imagine a mistake in a junction box that I'd be able to just brush off as harmless. So... I don't know. If I could somehow accept that, maybe the checking would go down, but getting my head to believe it is safe is the part I'm stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check things over and over if you were certain that you had done everything correctly, but not necessarily perfectly?\n\nPatient: Well, if I was absolutely certain it was correct and safe, then no, I wouldn't keep checking it. Like I said, I don't care about it being perfect. It's not about making it look pretty. If I knew for a fact that it was wired right and wouldn't cause a fire, I'd pack up my tools and go home. The checking only happens because I can't shake that feeling that I missed something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you were convinced that your work was safe, even if it wasn't done in the most efficient or perfect way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don't care about efficiency or perfection. If I was 100 percent convinced it was safe, I'd stop checking immediately. That's the only thing that matters to me—just knowing it won't cause a fire. If I had that guarantee, I wouldn't look at it twice.\n\nDoctor: Would your need to check things over and over decrease if the electrical components were organized in a messy but safe way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it would. I mean... I think that's kind of what I was just getting at. How it looks really doesn't matter to me at all. It could be a complete rat's nest of wires in there, and as long as I knew for an absolute fact that it was safe and wouldn't spark... yeah, I wouldn't need to keep checking it. The messiness isn't the issue for me, it's just the fear of it being dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Would your need to check things over and over decrease if someone else checked them first and confirmed they were safe, even if not perfectly organized?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I usually work alone on my jobs, so I haven't really had a situation where someone else comes in behind me to check my work. I'm not sure if just hearing another guy say it's safe would be enough to stop me from looking at it myself. I just haven't really had that happen, so it's hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your main concern when checking your work is the possibility of something going wrong and causing harm, rather than the work not being done perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's never about being perfect. It's just... even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought keeps barging in on me. I start thinking that maybe I missed one loose neutral wire, or I left something in a way that could start a fire. I try to shake it off and just leave it at work, but it doesn't work. That fear just follows me all the way home."
